2007. WILLIAM JAMESON , feloniously forging and uttering an order for payment of 40 l. 5 s.; with intent to defraud Sir Peter Laurie, Knt., and others.
MESSRS. RYLAND and PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
BRYAN REYNOLDS . I am cashier of the Argyle-street branch of the Union Bank of London; the chief office is in Princes-street. On 17th Oct. the
prisoner came and presented this check—("Pay Messrs. Dakin 49 l. 4 s., and debit the same to my account. WILLIAM H. ACRETT.") Mr. Acrett was a customer—I had misgivings about the check, and showed it to the manager—we both spoke to the prisoner. Mr. Clark asked where he got it—he said" From Dakin's, St. Paul's Churchyard"—he was given into custody.
WILLIAM GODFREY ( policeman, C 147). I took the prisoner—he said be told the gentleman he had the check from Dakin's, but that was false—I asked where he had it; he said, "From a man in the neighbourhood of Golden-square"—I asked who it was; he said he did not know, no more than he had seen him occasionally before, but did not know his name, or where he lived—I asked him if he wrote it—he said no, but he believed it to be a forgery when he presented it.
WILLIAM HENRY ACRETT . I am a surgeon, of Torrington-square—I keep an account at the Argyle-street branch of the Union Bank of London—this check is not drawn or signed by me or by anybody by my authority—I have seen the prisoner when he was assistant to a chemist in my neighbourhood.
ALEXANDER MAITLAND . I am a chemist, of Torrington-place. The prisoner was my assistant up to 28th March—I know his writing as well as I know his person—the whole of this check is his—I have hundreds of his entries in my prescription-book.
Prisoner. I did not say I believed it to be a forgery, but that I was fearful it was; I had my doubts about it; it is not my writing.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18491029-2008]
2008. CHARLES LOWES , GEORGE HARRIS , and WILLIAM THOMPSON , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Harvey, and stealing 1 watch and chain, value 3 l. 10 s.; 1 sovereign, and 7 s.; the property of Henry Harvey.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN HARVEY . I live at Sharp's-buildings, St. James's, Clerken-well; it is my husband's dwelling-house. On 9th Oct., about eight minutes to one, the prisoners came to my house—Harris asked for some dinner—they went up-stairs into the public room on the first-floor—(I knew Lowes and Harris before; I had seen them the day before, in front of the bar; they did not go up-stairs that day)—in about a quarter of an hour I sent my servant up to see that all was safe—she came down and told me something—I went up, and when I got on the first-floor I said to the prisoners," You good for nothing creatures, if you rob a poor woman who has to work as hard for a living as I do, you will rob anybody"—Harris and Thompson were sitting at the table, and Lowes leaning at the back window—Lowes said, "Stuff and nonsense"—I went to the second-floor and found the bed-room door and three drawers open; they all had been locked at eleven o'clock and the key was in my pocket—I missed a sovereign, seven shillings, and a watch and chain of my husband's worth 3 l. or 4 l.; this is it ( produced)—the prisoners were given in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What is your husband's name? A. Henry; he lives there.
Cross-examined by MR. M'MAHON. Q. Are there not two doors to your house 1 A. Yes; persons cannot get to the second-floor without being seen from the bar.
Cross-examined by MR. COCKLE. Q. It was Lowes and Harris you saw on the Monday? A. Yes.
ELLEN DONOVAN . I am Mrs. Harvey's servant—I took up some ale and
tobacco to the prisoners on the first-floor, and left them—about a quarter of an hour afterwards I went up towards the bed-room door, and heard a whistle on the first-floor—I saw Lowes coming out of the bed-room—he said " Lor, I have made a mistake and gone into the wrong room"—I said, " Lor, have you? "and he came down and went into the public room—I informed my mistress—there was no one there but the prisoners.
Cross-examined by MR. M'MAHON. Q. Had you been into the bed-room that morning before? A. No; it was after I passed the public room that I heard the whistle—the stairs face the door; they could see me going up.
THOMAS SOLOMON ( policeman, G 54). I took the prisoners—I found a bunch of skeleton keys on Lowes, one of which opens the bed-room door, and another the drawers—they would not givetheir names—they all said they had no address," Put down what you like."
JOHN PORTER ( policeman, G 101). I found this watch and chain, a sovereign, and seven shillings under the seat in the first-floor room at Mrs. Harvey's—the sovereign was wrapped in a little piece of paper.
LOWES — GUILTY .*— Aged 30.
HARRIS — GUILTY .*— Aged 33.
Transported for Ten Years.
THOMPSON — GUILTY.*—  Aged 33.
[trialAccount t18491029-2009]
2009. CHARLES BAINES , feloniously killing and slaying Mary Ann Maine; he was also charged on the Coroner's inquisition with the like offence.
MESSRS. HUDDLESTON and COCKLE conducted the Prosecution,
ELIZA ANDREWS , Jun. I am eleven years old, and live with my mother at 5, Hinton-street, Bethnal-green. I know the prisoner—he lived at our house with a woman that was called his wife—on Wednesday night, 26th Sept., I was called up-stairs a little after ten o'clock—I saw Mrs. Baines standing on the landing—the prisoner was in his room, lying on the bed with his clothes on—Mrs. Baines asked me to get her clothes; I went in, got them off a chair, and took them to her—she said to the prisoner, "I will let you know when I put on my clothes," and he got up and took away her clothes from her, put them on the bed, and lay on them; he then got off the bed again, came on the landing, and kicked her twice—I saw him do it—she was at the top of the stairs—he kicked her in the stomach the first time—she staggered back and held by the bannisters—he had his boots on at the time; they were bluchers—the second time he kicked her it was on the breast—she put her head down on the stairs and cried, and he went and laid on the bed again—she had no clothes on—she was lying down when she received the second kick—she lay down when he first kicked her—she did not lie there a minute, for I asked her if I should help her down, and she came down with me into my mother's room, the front room; she walked down—she then sat on a chair and cried—she asked me to help her down on the floor; I did, and she laid on the floor in our room and groaned—my mother was out—I asked if I should fetch anybody, and I fetched Mrs. Marling, the person next door; and after Mrs. Marling came in my mother came home—the prisoner was given into custody that same night—my mother called him down stairs; she called him three times before he came—Mrs. Baines had been packing up her things during the day; she was going to move, and my mother also—her crockery was packed up in a basket in her room; I saw one piece lying on the floor.
Prisoner. Q. What did Mrs. Baine call me when she got up? what did she have in her hand? A. Nothing.
CATHERINE MARLING . I am the wife of Thomas Marling, of 4, Hinton street. On Wednesday night, 26th Sept., I was sent for to the deceased's house—I went into Mrs. Andrews' room, and saw the deceased there—she was in very great agony, lying on the floor—I assisted her to Mrs. Andrew's bed, from which she was afterwards removed.
ELIZA ANDREWS . I lived at 5, Hinton-street, at the time in question—I came home on 26th, about ten minutes to eleven o'clock, and found the deceased lying on my bed, groaning dreadfully—I called the prisoner down stairs—after some time he came—he kissed his wife, and said something to her in a low tone, which I could not understand—he asked me if I could do anything for her, and I think he asked her to go up-stairs—she said, "I can't"—after that, he said something to her, and she called him a brute—I do not know that he made any answer to that, but he went on and said she was not hurt, she was only shamming, that she did it to collect a mob—he said nothing about the injury, nor did she in his presence—I went with her to the hospital.
DAVID DALE ( policeman, K 425). About half-past twelve o'clock on this night I was sent for, and saw the deceased lying on the bed in Mrs. Andrews's room—she appeared to be in a very dangerous state, groaning and in great pain—Mrs. Andrews sent up-stairs for the prisoner—he came down, went up to the deceased, kissed her, and asked if he could assist her to the hospital—she said, "Oh, you brute!"—he went and sat down again, and said she was a bthing, she was only shamming to colleet a mob about her—I did not bear anything said as to the cause of the injury—another constable took her in a cab to the hospital—I took the prisoner to the station—he said he had come home very tipsy, that his wife said something to him which he did not like, and a quarrel ensued, and he kicked her.
CHARLES HARPER . I am house-surgeon at the London Hospital, On Thursday morning, 27th Sept., the deceased was brought there—I attended her until her death, which was on the 28th, thirteen hours after her admission—I examined her when she first came—I found no external marks of violence on the abdomen—she was very ill, and suffering a great deal of pain in the abdomen—she seemed better until towards night, when she got worse, and gradually sank—I examined the body after death, I found the membranes of the intestines as if inflammation had taken place in them—I should suppose it to have been of about two days' standing—I also found slight disease of the lungs, but I attached no importance to that as it respects the cause of death; it was of very old standing—I found all the other organs of the body comparatively healthy, at least there was nothing about them to account for death—I attribute the death to peritonitis, or inflammation of the membrane covering the intestines—a kick with a boot would cause that—I should say the inflammation was of about two days' standing, at any rate very short; I could not say the number of hours, I should say between one and two days—I have heard the evidence—I should think an injury inflicted on the 26th might have caused what I saw on the 28th—it would depend very much on the condition of the deceased, and the intensity of the inflammation—I should say she had been in a sickly state before.
COURT? Q, Could the inflammation you saw have existed if it was produced by a kick, without there being some external mark? A. Certainly, from the elasticity of the part.
CATHERINE MOODY . I am the wife of John Moody, a sailor—my daughter lived with the prisoner—she was the wife of Charles Mayne, a ship scraper—her name was Mary Ann—I saw her dead body—it was the same person.
Prisoner's Defence ( written.) "I returned from work between eight and nine o'clock, in liquor, and found the deceased in bed; I had been cohabiting with her for twelve months. I went to her and said, 'Well, old girl, how are you?' she said, 'You b----wh----'s son you shall not stop here; get out of bed;' and she laid hold of a basin, with the intention, as I thought, of throwing it at me; I told her she had better be quiet; I turned to go to bed, when she struck me on the right ear; and in the passion of the moment I kicked out at her, not thinking she was within reach; but, unfortunately she was. I beg for mercy."
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years.

2011. THOMAS CONNELL was again indicted for unlawfully assaulting Sarah Ware, with intent, &c.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Six Months.
OLD COURT.— Wednesday, October 31st, 1849.
PRESENT —Mr. RECORDER and Mr. Ald. FARNCOMB.
Before Mr. Recorder and the First Jury.
[trialAccount t18491029-2012]
2012. RICHARD MATLEY SMITH , THOMAS LAWES , and CHARLES FRASER unlawfully obtaining 7 soverigns and 1 half-sovereign; the moneys of Francis Cuthbertson, by false pretences:—2d COUNT, for a conspiracy.
MR. M'MAHON conducted the Prosecution
FRANCIS CUTHBERTSON . I am a bootmaker, and live at 123, Aldersgate-street, City. On Tuesday, 28th Aug., in consequence of a message which I received, I went to the Clarence Hotel, and in the coffee-room there I saw the prisoner, Smith, and a Mr. Collins, in his company—Captain Smith, as he called himself, stated to me that the landlord had gone out of town, or he would have given him the money for this check ( producing it)—I unfortunately knew Smith for a few weeks before, as a customer—I looked at the check, and finding it was made payable to Mr. F. Collins, who was in Smith's company, and his companion, and knowing Mr. Collins to be a man of property, and as it was drawn payable to him, I supposed it was a good instru ment, and immediately took out my purse and gave Smith 7 l. 10 s. for it; that is the amount the check is drawn for—I did not at that time perceive that the check was post-dated, the circumstance of its being crossed was the only thing that induced me to give them the money without its going through a banker—Smith alluded to its being crossed, and said, as the check was crossed, would I give him the money for it, as the landlord was out of town—I did not discover that it was post-dated till next day, when it was going to be sent to the bankers; Smith never alluded to it—I afterwards charged him with giving me a post-dated check—he did not explain the circumstance at all—the only explanation I had was, that
Fraser, the man that drew the check, had the cholera; and then that he had died—the first time I saw him after he gave me the check was on the 3d the day before it was due—he then wished me not to pay it in, as Fraser was ill of the cholera, and could not go to Gravesend—I said I certainly should pay it in, and let it go through the bankers—my brother paid it on the 3d, with other moneys, and it was returned to me on the 5th—I afterwards went to the Commercial Bank; I found they had moved from 3, Moorgate-street, more than twelve months before, and they told me they knew nothing of such parties—I afterwards saw Smith, and spoke to him about the check being returned—he said that Fraser was dead of the cholera—I told him he had given me a forged check—he said it would be paid, I could go to Mr. Lawes, his solicitor, in Moorgate-street, who gave him the check—we had a long conversation—I saw him several times—I afterwards went to Lawes, in Moorgate-street; I told him that I had a forged check, and that Captain Smith had sent me to him; that he told me he had received it from him—I produced it to him—Lawes told me that Fraser had a rich brother at Gravesend, and there had been some misunderstanding, which was the reason the money had not been paid; he did not pay it—I told him Smith said Fraser was dead—he said, "Oh!" and put it off in an au fait, easy sort of way—he said, never mind that, "Come in and see Mr. Hall," that was a gentleman in the front office—I did so; and Mr. Hall stated to me, in Lawes presence, that Fraser's brother was a man of property at Gravesend, and it would ultimately be settled, and he thought I should get the money on the following Saturday; Hall said that Lawes should come to pay the check on the Saturday, and Lawes said it certainly should be paid on Saturday—neither Lawes or any one else came to pay it on the Saturday; it has not yet been paid.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you known Collins before? A. I had; he is a son of Mr. Collins, the grazier, a man of great respectability—seeing Collins name to the check, and his giving it me in Collins' presence, gave me a degree of confidence in it—I cannot say what I should have done if I had not seen his name to it—I cannot say whether his name induced me to pay it—I had no doubt about it being a real transaction, and a good instrument—when I laid the money on the table, Smith took it, and said to Collins," Here Collins, we halve the money when we get it; you take half, and I will take half"—that opened my eyes, but the money was gone then—I have not applied to Collins for the payment of the check—I have applied to Smith, in Collins' presence—I believe Smith's relations are respectable—he has been accustomed to attend the Carence for some years—he has, unfortunately, spent all his own, and other people's money; there are other checks the subject of inquiry—his father is dead—I am not quite sure whether he was a Magistrate.
MR. M'MAHON. Q. Do you know whether he paid his bill at the Clarence? A. The landlord told me he owed him 100 l.
JAMES BECK . I am clerk at the Gravesend Bank, and have been so nearly six years. We have no customer of the name of Charles Fraser—we had not on 4th Sept., nor ever had.
Cross-examined. Q. Supposing any person were to pay cash, say 50 l., and ask you to let it pass through your hands and be made payable to your agents in town, should you allow it to be done? A. Yes; it does not require that the person should be in our books—we would take a single sum of a stranger—the Commercial Bank are correspondents of ours—it is, in fact, the same house, for the purpose of transmitting money; we should
charge a commission on it—if we bad funds for the purpose, we should send money to the Commercial Bank to enable them to pay the person who presented the check—the sum of 7 l. 10 s. was not paid in on 3d Sept.; there have been no entries of it in our books—I am not the sole person who takes money—it could not be done without my knowing it, because I examined the book—if the clerk who received it entered it properly, I should see it.
MATTHEW KIGHT . I keep the British coffee-house, Blackfriars-road. On Tuesday, 25th Sept., a young man of respectable appearance brought a desk to my house, and asked me if I would oblige him by letting it rem in till the afternoon—I afterwards saw that young man speaking to Lawes at the police-court—I gave the desk to Russell.
GEORGE RUSSELL ( City-policeman, 34). On Tuesday afternoon, 25th Sept., I went to Kight's coffee-house—I received this desk ( produced)—Kight was present—I found these papers and books in it ( produced).
ARTHUR GEORGE HALL . I am a clerk in the Commercial Bank of London. The offices are now in Lothbury—they were removed there in 1840 or 1841, I believe, but I have only been there three or four years—I know Lawes as a former customer—the last transaction he had with us was on 13th April, 1848, as far as my memory serves—this check-book ( produced; found in the desk) is one of ours—no doubt he had a check-book when he had an account with us—I have seen the writing on the counterfoils in this check-book—I believe it to be Lawes' to the best of my belief—this memorandum book ( produced; also from the desk) is the one that was issued to Lawes when he was a customer, and this pass-book ( also from the desk) was issued to him—the entries in this memorandum-book appear to be in Lawes' writing—this check was presented for payment on 4th Sept., and was refused—we have no customer named Charles Fraser.
Cross-examined. Q. You have change'd the colouring and lithographing of your check-books since my client had the book? A. Yes; these two checks are totally different, but still they belong to the same bank—I do not suppose they came out of this book—we sometimes pay post-dated checks by accident—I can say I did not pay a check of 12 l. 10 s. for Mr. Lawes' on 4th May, 1849—I inspected the ledger this morning, and Lawes' account seemed to be almost closed—the account was overdrawn—I do not recollect how much—he began with 200 l.
JOHN HARVEY ( policeman, G 14). I apprehended Lawes in Milk-street, on 22d Sept.—I afterwards took Fraser, searched him, and found these papers on him ( marked A and B.)
HENRY FLIGHT . I am a share-broker. I know Fraser, and have seen two or three letters from him—the writing in these memorandum-books, "A" and" B," I believe to be his.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you ever seen Fraser write? A. No, I merely judge from three or four letters of inquiry I have received, purporting to come from Fraser—I have not acted upon those letters—I talked to Fraser about them. (The following memorandums were read from the books, "Received of Thomas Lawes for drawing two checks, and received to account 1 s., leaving 1 s. still due by that gentleman, 29th Aug." "Received 3d Sept., of Mr. Thomas Lawes, the balance of 1 s. on account of commission.") MR. M'MAHON. Q. Whose writing do you believe the signature to the check to be? A. It don't look to me like Fraser's writing—I cannot swear to it—it does not appear to be at all like it—I do not believe it is his—I do not know the writing of the body—I know none of the writing of the check—I
have no belief about it—Fraser said nothing to me about the check—I do not know the other prisoners at all.
WILLIAM HALL re-examined. I do not know the writing of the body—I know Lawes' writing—I do not believe the body is Lawes' writing—I do not know Fraser's writing.
FREDERICK COLLINS . I was in the room when the check was handed to Mr. Cuthbertson—I cannot say whether I was in the same box—I do not know the writing of any part of the check—I believe the check is given in my favour—my name is F. Collins.
Cross-examined. Q. You have no doubt that the check was drawn in your favour, have you? A. Well, I cannot say—I believe Smith called me when the check was cashed; I was in another part of the room—while the money was one the table, Smith took some of the money and I some, but how much I cannot exactly say; it could not have been exactly half—I might have had pretty near half of it—I took it because Smith owed me money, and I supposed the check was obtained by honourable means—I did not know it was made payable to me—I had not seen it before—I swear that—I afterwards saw Lawes, and he gave me the money to pay the check, when the case went before the Clerkenwell police-court, that was on a Saturday—he did not give me the money before I appeared, because I went up there first to ask if it was correct—I have the money now—I did not pay it over—I was not allowed to do so—I was given to understand by somebody about the Court, I cannot say exactly who, that it could not be settled, as it had been once, before the Magistrate—the holder of the check never applied to me for it, and so I could not have done it—it was not about this check at all, it was about Mr. Mills' check, that was in the same way—I mean to return the 7 l. 10 s.—I have not done it, because I have had no application; it will be done—I have no wish to hold another man's money—I was to pay it to Mr. Mills.
Q. Now, on your oath, were you not desired to pay 7 l. 10 s. to Mr.
Cuthbertson, I only want an answer, yes or no, and just be cautious? A. Well, I cannot state which way it was now, the two transactions have been so mixed up together—I swear 7 l. 10 s. is all the money I have received for the purpose of paying the parties—that is not all the money I have received of Lawes since 3d Aug.—he did some private business for me—I do not know how much I have received—I have no account—I have not received more than 7 l. 10 s. from him to pay checks—I have not received 15 l. from him since 28th Aug.—I received the 7 l. 10 s. on the Saturday, and I do not remember that I have received any other sum of him since 28th Aug.—I may have received money on my private account; but not to my recollection—I fancy it was previous to that—it was not 50 l., I should say it was between 20 l. and 30 l.—I swear that was not for the purpose of paying the two 7 l. 10 s. checks—I did not undertake to pay the other 7 l. 10 s.—the private accounts between us were about some money Lawes had got for me, for which I gave him a bill, which I shall have to take up at the end of this month—I think it is for 30 l.—I suppose I received between 20 l. to 30 l. from him; but I cannot tell—besides that, I have received nothing else except the 7 l. 10 s.
COURT. Q. Was your name on Mills's check? A. Yes; I saw it at the police-court—I did not see it before Mills had it.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Of course you have paid back the money to the prosecutor, the half of the amount of this check? A. No; I had reason to
believe the account would be made all square with the prosecutor, as far as I was concerned—that was not in order to keep the 7 l. 10 s. in my own pocket—I have not got it in my pocket now; I can send for it. COURT. Q. They have not indicted you? A. They have not. MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you got 7 l. 10 s. in the world? A. I have at Pocklington's and Laceys, in Smithfield, my trustees keep my accounts—I can have money by applying to them for it—I cannot draw anything out myself.
COURT. Q. What has become of the 7 l. 10 s.? A. God knows; I can repay it at any time—I suppose it has been spent; since that, money has been obtained by another party, and I did not think this would be brought in question—I have paid away the money, and I have had a party receiving that money through some rather indirect transaction—I would rather not mention names; it would not criminate myself, perhaps I might say more than I should wish; there is nothing very particular about it, but perhaps it might go further into private matters than I should like—it might involve me in another transaction; I do not know whether it would involve me in an indictment.
MR. M'MAHON. Q. Where did you receive the 7 l. 10 s. from Laws? A. At the Dolphin, in Milk-street; an officer was with me; Lawes was not in custody—it was on the Saturday we went to the police-court, I forget the day of the month—I went from the court to Lawes, to inquire whether there was anything not fair or fraudulent in the transaction—I told him that Smith was then in custody, and I should like him to come up and see whether anything could be done in it.
COURT. Q. Did you give any part of the 20 l. or 30 l., you received from Lawes to Smith? A. I lent him some money, Smith bad taken up the transaction on my behalf; at least I let him do the dirty work, going backwards and forwards—he received it for me one day, and brought it me, and had some of it: we were pals together—he was not my agent to raise the money, and we were rather more intimate than I liked—I am not yet twenty years old.
SMITH — GUILTY on the 1st Count.. Aged 29.— Confined One Year.
LAWES and FRASER — NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18491029-2013]
2013. THOMAS LAWES and CHARLES FRASER , were again indicted for unlawfully obtaining 6 l. 15 s. of George Mills, by false pretences.—2d COUNT, for a conspiracy.
MR. M'MAHON conducted the Prosecution
WILLIAM HUXLEY . I am ostler, at the Clarence Hotel. On Monday, 3d Sept., Smith sent me to Mr. Mills, of Goswell-street, to get a dog-cart for two or three days—he gave me this check ( produced) to pay for the hire of it, and to bring him the difference—I went to Mr. Mills and told him I had come to hire a dog-cart for Mr. Smith, of the Clarence Hotel—I gave him the check, and told him to. take the three days' hire out of it—he gave me the difference, 6 l. 15 s.—I went back and saw Smith in the Clarence-yard, and gave it to him—I saw the dog-cart brought to the yard.
Cross-examined by MR. COCKLE. Q. I believe Mr. Smith went to Richmond for three or four days, after that? A. I believe so—he made use of the dog-cart.
GEORGE MILLS . I am a coach-maker, of 171, Goswell-street. On 3d Sept., I received this check from Huxley—not having the cash by me, I drew a check for the difference, 6 l. 15 s., and gave it to Huxley—I did not at that time notice the date of this check—I paid it into the London and Westminster
Bank next day, the 4th, and on the 5th received it back—it was dated 11th—I have received my own check of 6 l. 15 s. back from my bankers—I have not received any payment for this check since—I presented it at the Commercial Bank of London on 11th Sept., and the answer was, that there was no account open in such a name, and that in their opinion it was a forgery.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose Mr. Collins has not paid you any part of it? A. I did not know Mr. Collins—I never saw him till the present transaction.
WILLIAM HUXLEY re-examined, I got this check for 6 l. 15 s. from Mr. Mills, got it cashed at a public-house opposite our hotel, and gave the amount to Mr. Smith.
Cross-examined. Q. When Mr. Smith gave you the first check to take to Mr. Mills, did he not say it was post-dated? A. If he did I did not hear him—he was talking something, but I was ordered off in a hurry to Barnet-fair, and did not notice what he said—I could not swear he did not tell me it was post-dated—I do not know what he said.
MATTHEW KIGHT . I keep the British Coffee-house in Blackfriars-road. On Tuesday, 25th Sept., I received this desk from a young man, who I afterwards saw speaking to Lawes at the police-court—I gave it up to Russell that same afternoon, as I received it.
GEORGE RUSSELL ( City-policeman, 34). On 25th Sept., I went to Kight and received from him this desk—it then contained the papers and documents that are here now.
ARTHUR GEORGE HALL . I have been, since Jan. 1845, a clerk in the Commercial Bank of London. On 4th Sept., this check was paid to us by the London and Westminster Bank—it was first paid by us to the London and Westminster Bank, but we found out our error and had it back—on searching I found that Charles Fraser, the drawer, had no account at our bank—we do business for the Gravesend Bank—I believe this check has come out of this check-book ( produced from the desk)—I cannot point out from what place, but the numbers agree—the counterfoils all bear the same number, 5676—the number of this check is 567—the last figure 6 has been removed—I know Lawes; he once kept an account at our bank—this check-book was issued by us to him—I believe the writing on the counterfoils to be his—the body of this check I also believe to be his—I mean the words that are written "Sept 11, '49. J. F. Collins, Esq., 7 l. 10 s. on account of the Gravesend Bank," and the figures" 7 l. 10 s." all the writing part, except Fraser's signature—this was our pass-book with Lawes—I believe the entries in this memorandum-book to be Lawes's writing—the last entry is "1849, Aug. 28. Cash per Smith, 2 l. 10 s."—I cannot say when Lawes's account with us ceased—the last transaction I am aware of was on 13th April.
Cross-examined Q. Have all these books the same number of checks in them? A. No; they begin at 25 and go up to 500—I have not counted the checks in this book to see whether any are missing—I feel no difficulty in speaking to the body of this check being in Lawes's writing, from the knowledge I have of it—I should say it is the same character of writing as that on the counterfoils—in my opinion all the body of this check, except the signature, is in one handwriting—there is a slight difference in the colour of the ink with which" Pay to F. Collins" is written. JOSEPH BEADLE. I am a clerk in the Commercial Bank of London.
I produce the book in which is recorded the check-books issued to customers—I find that the number of the check-book issued to Lawes was 5676—the entry is in my own writing.
JAMES BECK . I am a clerk in the Gravesend Bank, and have been so five or six years. During that time no such person as Charles Fraser has kept any account at our bank—there was no money to the credit of such a name OD 4th or 11th Sept. last, nor at any time.
Cross-examined. Q. You do receive money paid in by strangers? A. Yes and pay those sums in London through the Commercial bank.
JOHN HARVEY ( policeman, G 14). I apprehended Lawes in Milk-street, on 22d Sept., about half-past two o'clock—I told him the charge, and said be must go to Clerkenwell police-court with me about Smith's business respecting a check—he said he would be dif he would—he said be gave
Smith the check—he then said that Fraser signed the check, and he ought to go as well—I asked where Fraser was—he said on the Exchange, and I went with him to the Exchange—I there found Fraser—I told him I wanted him to go to Clerkenwell police-station—he wanted to know what for—I told him about a check—Lawes then said, "You know you signed your name to that check"—he said, "Yes"—I believe Smith's name was mentioned by a person named Hall—Fraser said if I would wait half an hour he would go, when he had finished his business—I then took him and Lawes in a cab to the station—a person named Hall was also in the cab, he represented himself as being an attorney in Moorgate-street—I did not take him into custody, he went of his own accord—as they were going along Lawes said to Fraser, "Charlie, if the Magistrate should ask you why you gave a check on the Gravesend Bank, tell him you expected the money to be paid in immediately, "and Fraser said" Yes"—I examined Lawes at the station, and found on him this pocket-book, containing these two blank checks (numbered 5676), a 10 l.-note, and a quantity of papers—I also searched Fraser, and found on him the papers marked" A "and" B"—I was before the Magistrate when Collins was being examined ( looking at the deposition)—this is Mr. Combe's writing ( read —" The prisoner Fraser voluntarily states: The signature to the check is in my handwriting; only the signature is in my handwriting; I do not know anything about the rest of the handwriting ")—I heard that stated—Lawes afterwards made a statement—he said, "I believe all the handwriting on the check to be in Fraser's handwriting; none of it is mine"—Fraser said, "Most probably I signed the check at Garraway's; I have had transactions of this kind before, but such transactions were always met, and if there is anything wrong, come to me on Monday at twelve o'clock on the Exchange and it shall be all right"—Inspector Brannan is not here; he is ill of the cholera.
Cross-examined. Q. It was a 10 l. Bank of England note you found on Lawes? A. Yes; what he said to Fraser in the cab was nearly at the end of our ride—there had been a good deal of conversation—I think what Lawes said was, "You know you signed your name to that check"—I could not say positively that it was not," Did you sign your name to that check?"—I do not know which it was—both charges were not investigated on the first day—I am speaking of the 22nd—Smith had been taken on the night previous—the first charge was Mr. Mills's—the other check was not then produced—they were remanded, and Mr. Cuthbertson's check was brought forward on the second examination.
FREDERICK COLLINS . ( This witness was cautioned by the Court that he was not bound to answer any question that might criminate himself.) I cannot
minster Bank next day, the 4th, and on the 5th received it back—it was dated 11th—I have received my own check of 6 l. 15 s. back from my bankers—I have not received any payment for this check since—I presented it at the Commercial Bank of London on 11th Sept., and the answer was, that there was no account open in such a name, and that in their opinion it was a forgery.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose Mr. Collins has not paid you any part of it? A. I did not know Mr. Collins—I never saw him till the present transaction.
WILLIAM HUXLEY re-examined. I got this check for 6 l. 15 s. from Mr. Mills, got it cashed at a public-house opposite our hotel, and gave the amount to Mr. Smith.
Cross-examined. Q. When Mr. Smith gave you the first check to take to Mr. Mills, did he not say it was post-dated? A. If he did I did not hear him—he was talking something, but I was ordered off in a hurry to Barnet-fair, and did not notice what he said—I could not swear he did not tell me it was post-dated—I do not know what he said.
MATTHEW KIGHT . I keep the British Coffee-house in Black friars-road. On Tuesday, 25th Sept., I received this desk from a young man, who I afterwards saw speaking to Lawes at the police-court—I gave it up to Russell that same afternoon, as I received it.
GEORGE RUSSELL ( City-policeman, 34). On 25th Sept., I went to Kight and received from him this desk—it then contained the papers and documents that are here now.
ARTHUR GEORGE HALL . I have been, since Jan. 1845, a clerk in the Commercial Bank of London. On 4th Sept., this check was paid to us by the London and Westminster Bank—it was first paid by us to the London and Westminster Bank, but we found out our error and had it back—on searching I found that Charles Fraser, the drawer, had no account at our bank—we do business for the Gravesend Bank—I believe this check has come out of this check-book ( produced from the desk)—I cannot point out from what place, but the numbers agree—the counterfoils all bear the same number, 5676—the number of this check is 567—the last figure 6 has been removed—I know Lawes; he once kept an account at our bank—this check-book was issued by us to him—I believe the writing on the counterfoils to be his—the body of this check I also believe to be his—I mean the words that are written "Sept 11, '49. J. F. Collins, Esq., 7 l. 10 s. on account of the Gravesend Bank," and the figures "7 l. 10 s." all the writing part, except Fraser's signature—this was our pass-book with Lawes—I believe the entries in this memorandum-book to be Lawes's writing—the last entry is" 1849, Aug. 28. Cash per Smith, 2 l. 10 s."—I cannot say when Lawes's account with us ceased—the last transaction I am aware of was on 13th April.
Cross-examined Q. Have all these books the same number of checks in them? A. No; they begin at 25 and go up to 500—I have not counted the checks in this book to see whether any are missing—I feel no difficulty in speaking to the body of this check being in Lawes's writing, from the knowledge I have of it—I should say it is the same character of writing as that on the counterfoils—in my opinion all the body of this check, except the signature, is in one handwriting—there is a slight difference in the colour of the ink with which "Pay to F. Collins" is written. JOSEPH BEADLE. I am a clerk in the Commercial Bank of London.
I produce the book in which is recorded the check-books issued to customers—I find that the number of the check-book issued to Lawes was 5676—the entry is in my own writing.
JAMES BECK . I am a clerk in the Gravesend Bank, and have been so five or six years. During that time no such person as Charles Fraser has kept any account at our bank—there was no money to the credit of such a name on 4th or 11th Sept. last, nor at any time.
Cross-examined. Q. You do receive money paid in by strangers? A. Yes and pay those sums in London through the Commercial bank.
JOHN HARVEY ( policeman, G 14). I apprehended Lawes in Milk-street, on 22d Sept., about half-past two o'clock—I told him the charge, and said he must go to Clerkenwell police-court with roe about Smith's business respecting a check—he said he would be dif he would—he said he gave
Smith the check—he then said that Fraser signed the check, and he ought to go as well—I asked where Fraser was—he said on the Exchange, and I went with him to the Exchange—I there found Fraser—I told him I wanted him to go to Clerkenwell police-station—he wanted to know what for—I told him about a check—Lawes then said, "You know you signed your name to that check"—he said, "Yes"—I believe Smith's name was mentioned by a person named Hall—Fraser said if I would wait half an hour he would go, when he had finished his business—I then took him and Lawes in a cab to the station—a person named Hall was also in the cab, he represented himself as being an attorney in Moorgate-street—I did not take him into custody, he went of his own accord—as they were going along Lawes said to Fraser, "Charlie, if the Magistrate should ask you why you gave a check on the Gravesend Bank, tell him you expected the money to be paid in immediately, "and Fraser said" Yes"—I examined Lawes at the station, and found on him this pocket-book, containing these two blank checks (numbered 5676), a 10 l.-note, and a quantity of papers—I also searched Fraser, and found on him the papers marked" A "and" B"—I was before the Magistrate when Collins was being examined ( looking at the deposition)—this is Mr. Combe's writing ( read —" The prisoner Fraser voluntarily states: The signature to the check is in my handwriting; only the signature is in my handwriting; I do not know anything about the rest of the handwriting ")—I heard that stated—Lawes afterwards made a statement—he said, "I believe all the handwriting on the check to be in Fraser's handwriting; none of it is mine"—Fraser said, "Most probably I signed the check at Oarraway's; I have had transactions of this kind before, but such transactions were always met, and if there is anything wrong, come to me on Monday at twelve o'clock on the Exchange and it shall be all right"—Inspector Brannan is not here; he is ill of the cholera.
Cross-examined. Q. It was a 10 l. Bank of England note you found on Lawes? A. Yes; what he said to Fraser in the cab was nearly at the end of our ride—there had been a good deal of conversation—I think what Lawes said was, "You know you signed your name to that check"—I could not say positively that it was not," Did you sign your name to that check?"—I do not know which it was—both charges were not investigated on the first day—I am speaking of the 22nd—Smith had been taken on the night previous—the first charge was Mr. Mills's—the other check was not then produced—they were remanded, and Mr. Cuthbertson's check was brought forward on the second examination.
FREDERICK COLLINS . ( This witness was cautioned by the Court that he was not bound to answer any question that might criminate himself.) I cannot
remember whether I saw this check before 3d Sept. last—I do not remember seeing it before 3d—I do not think I saw it till it was brought into my bed-room one morning—that was after the 11th, or somewhere thereabout—the landlord of the hotel brought it into my room, and asked me if I knew anything about it—he told me that it was given to him by Mr. Mills—that was the first time I saw anything of it—I never saw it in the hands of Lawes, to my recollection—I think, as far as I can call to mind, that was the first time I saw it—I fancy it must have been after 11th Sept.—I think it was after it had been presented at the bank—I have seen it since at the police-court, but I do not remember seeing it before—I cannot positively state whether I saw it produced by Lawes to Smith at the Clarence before 11th Sept.—I might have done so, but not to my recollection.
Q. Did Lawes say, on producing it to Smith or you, in the presence of you both, "I shall give you this check if you can get anybody to cash it?" A. My Lord, am I justified in answering this question, as I am given to understand that perhaps any answer I make may be construed into some participation—I decline to answer the question—I decline answering whether Smith took up the check.
HENRY FLIGHT . I am clerk to Mr. Killick, a sharebroker. These entries in the memorandum-books, marked" A" and "B," I believe to be Fraser's writing ( these were read as before)—the signature, "Chas. Fraser," to this check appears similar to the last, but I could not swear to it—I do not believe it to be his, I cannot recognize it as such.
JOHN WHALE . I am a waiter at the Clarence Hotel. Smith staid there for three months, last autumn—Lawes occasionally called to see him, altogether about eight times.
Cross-examined. Q. He had not called for some months or six weeks previous to this transaction, had he? A. That was about the time—I think the last time was about three weeks or a month before they were taken into custody—I did not take particular notice of the time. (Lawes and Fraser received good characters.)
SMITH — GUILTY. Aged 29.— Confined One Year. LAWES — GUILTY . Aged 49. FRASER — GUILTY . Aged 50. Confined Eighteen Months.
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, Oct. 31st, 1849.
PRESENT —Mr. Ald. CARDEN; and EDWARD BULLOCK, Esq.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq. and the Seventh Jury.

2014. JOHN WILLIAMS , unlawfully breaking and entering the Church of St. Mary Somerset in London, with intent to steal.
SARAH BLACK MORE . I keep the keys of St. Mary Somerset Church, Fish-street-hill. I went there on 24th Oct., about two o'clock in the afternoon, and found the lock wrenched off the vestry door, and the door open—I had left it locked on Monday, between twelve and one—I went into the Church from the vestry, and saw the prisoner asleep in one of the pews, covered with cushions—I should not have seen him if I had not removed them—a window was broken.
RICHARD JOHN SIMPSON SERGO . I am Rector of St. Mary Somerset. On 24th Oct. I ordered Mrs. Blackmore to open the vestry for me—the lock
was wrenched, and the door open—one of the Church candles had been burned, and lucifers were lying about the vestry—a pane of glass was broken in the window, next to the fastening—I saw the prisoner found, and fetched the Churchwarden and a policeman—the poker and some iron had been removed from the vestry.
JAMES STREET ( City-policeman, 476). I was sent for, and found the prisoner lying in a pew, covered with cushions—his boots were off—he had a box of lucifers and this knife, which had been recently sharpened, in his pocket—this stone was lying on the pew beside him—I found this poker and piece of iron and gas key hid in the dust-bin—the marks on the vestry door correspond with this iron. GUILTY . — Confined Twelve Months.

[trialAccount t18491029-2016]
2016. WILLIAM CRANBY , stealing 51lbs. weight of lead, value 5 s.; the goods of Nicholas Charrington and others.
MR. COCKLE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WILLIAM MANNING ( policeman, K 5). In consequence of information, I went to the house of Moses Johnson, at Stratford, at half-past seven o'clock in the evening, on the 12th Oct.—he produced this lead, and from what he said, I went to "The Pigeons," and saw the prisoner—I asked him if he knew anything of any lead—he said, "No"—I repeated the question, and he said, "What lead do you mean, the lead in the stable?"—I said, "Have you sold any lead?"—he said, "No "two or three times—I took him to How station, and he looked down at this lead in the charge-room, and said, "It is no use saying I did not take the lead, I did; I took it off the roof of the stable at the back of Mrs. Laythorpe's"—I compared this lead with some in the stable, and it corresponded exactly with the lead of the scullery, and with the lead of the gutter called "the drip."
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Did you not follow Johnson from the stable to his own house? A. I did not, I had never been in the stable before—I know the roof of the stable—I cannot say how long it is ago since new lead was put there—I should say it is about three months—I knew Mr. Laythorpe before he died—he has been dead above three months—I think the lead was not put on before he died—I think Mrs. Laythorpe is not here, I saw her here yesterday—I think the prisoner has been ostler there about two
years—I have been on that beat four years—the prisoner has not lived there all that time, he lived in Leytonstone before that—I did not ask him if he had stolen any lead.
SAMUEL SAXBY . I am a zinc-worker, and reside at Stratford—I accompanied the officer to "The Pigeons," and compared this lead produced with other lead in the stable and scullery—they corresponded—I found marks in the lead corresponding with the drip on the roof—this piece came off the gutter—here are marks where it had been dressed over the drip.
Cross-examined. Q. In whose employ are you? A. Mr. Little's—I repaired the gutter with zinc, in August—I was told to remove the lead, and to put zinc—there had been a portion of the lead taken away before—I threw the lead off the roof, and gave it to the ostler—that was the last I saw of it—I never sent to take it away—I threw it down outside the stable, and left it—I was told there was other lead there.
ALFRED HEAD . I am a partner of Nicholas Charrington and others—we are lessees of" The Pigeons."
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know anything about when this other old lead was taken off the stable? A. The gutter had been stripped some months before, under my direction—if the prisoner had not taken the lead, our surveyor would have sent to remove it—I cannot tell when he ought to have sent for it—I think he did send for it some time afterwards, and could not find it—our surveyor charges so much for what he does to the houses—the old lead is not his perquisite, it would come back to the brewery.
MOSES JOHNSON . I am a marine-store dealer. On 12th Oct. I purchased about 50lbs. of lead of the prisoner, I gave him 3 s. 6 d. for it, and stood a quartern of gin—I saw him going down Stratford—he said he had two or three pieces of lead, if I liked to buy them—I went and bought them, and brought them away.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was this? A. Between five and six o'clock in the evening—I have lived there about three years and a half-there were persons about the yard.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Two Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.

[trialAccount t18491029-2018]
2018. THOMAS EADE , stealing 500 carrots, value 9 s.; the goods of William Adams, his master.
JAMES CHAMBERS . I am foreman to William Adams, of West Ham, Essex. On 14th Oct., 1848, the prisoner was in his service, and was sent to town with sixty dozen of carrots, in a wagon, to Mr. Gregory, of Spitalfields-market—I made out a note of the quantity, and the carter's name; he was the prisoner—I left it with Cordery, the watchman, to give to the prisoner—the prisoner had no authority to deliver any of the carrots on the road, but to take them to the salesman—I did not see him start or come back—I did not see him again till he was in custody, nearly twelve months after—he was in regular employ—he never came back to his master—the carrots were Mr. Adams's property until delivered to Mr. Gregory, for sale.
RICHARD IMRAY . I live at Saville-road, Mile-end-road. On 14th Oct., 1848, about half-past three in the morning, I was walking to town, and as I passed the Fountain public-house, in the Mile-end-road, I saw a wagon with carrots in it, and saw the prisoner and two other persons unloading the carrots into the Fountain stable—the prisoner was on the wagon, and the two others carrying them into the stable—I afterwards saw the prisoner go on with the wagon—about three-quarters of an hour afterwards I went with a police-man to the stable, and saw the carrots there—he took charge of them, and I gave information to Mr. Gregory.
Prisoner. Q. How could you tell me from the other men by that light? A. The shutter was down at the Fountain, and the gas shone on you.
EDWARD ABRAMS . I am foreman to Mr. Gregory, a salesman of Spitalfields-market. On the morning of the 15th the prisoner brought a wagon of carrots there—there were six or seven wagons came that morning from Mr. Gregory, and one man brought a note of the whole—the prisoner left his wagon, took an empty one, and went to load some dung—Mr. Imray then gave me information—I examined the wagon, and found the carrots had been disturbed, and there was a space where some had been taken from—I then went to the stable at the Fountain, and found three dozen and two, bunches of carrots, which I compared with the others and found them to be the same—Mr. Gregory sells entirely for Mr. Adams—I can swear they were Mr. Adams's carrots—we told over the remainder of the carrots, and there were three dozen and three punches short—I went after the prisoner to Theobald's-road, where he was to load the dung, and found the wagon and four horses there, and no driver—I did not see him again till this Oct.
CHARLES ROWBURY ( policeman, K 347). I received information, and looked after the prisoner, but did not find him till 19th last Sept, when I saw him on top of a wagon in Stratford—I took him into custody—he said if he had done anything wrong he must suffer for it.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.

2027. MARY MULCHNOCK , stealing 2 shillings; the moneys of George Vere Goldfinch, her master.
GEORGE VERE GOLDFINCH . I am a linen-draper, at Deptford; the prisoner was in my service. For some reason, on 27th Sept. I marked four sixpences, nine shillings, and two half-crowns, and put them in the till—I went to tea, and the prisoner came to mind the shop, which was part of her duty—I left no one in the shop but her—I went back into the shop in half an hour, and she immediately left—I then went to the till and missed two shillings—I went down-stairs, called the prisoner from the wash-house, and said, "You have taken two shillings out of the till"—she said she had not, and I might search her—I called a policeman, and had her taken in charge—he afterwards produced these two marked shillings to me ( produced)—they are two of those I put in the till.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Who lives in the house besides you? A. My mother and a little boy, my sister's child, about nine years old—he is not here—I went into the kitchen to tea—my mother occasionally serves in the shop—there was only marked money in the till—my mother, brother, his wife, and the little boy and I had tea together—when I went back from tea, I left the others down-stairs.
EDWARD BENJAMIN SCOTT ( policeman, R 71). I took the prisoner into custody, and took her to the station—I then went back to the prosecutor's, and found the two shillings I have produced in a beading just over the sink.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18491029-2028]
2028. CHARLES HINCHEY , stealing 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 s.; the goods of Richard Parry and another: having been before convicted.
CHARLES DAVIS . I am salesman to Richard Parry, of Deptford, clothier. On 1st Oct., between five and six o'clock I missed a pair of trowsers from the door-post, which I had seen safe half an hour previous—these are them ( produced)— they are Richard Parry and Son's property.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman, R 180). On 1st Oct. I was on duty in Deptford—I saw the prisoner, with two other boys, loitering about Mr. Parry's shop about three-quarters of an hour—they then went to the shop, and after remaining a short time, left—I turned round, and saw the prisoner putting something under his coat; he was then about five yards from the shop—I pursued him, and just before I overtook him, he dropped this pair of trowsers—he said, "I did not take them"—I took him to the station.
Prisoner's Defence. My father won't keep me; it was not me that stole them.
DAVID OVENDEN ( policeman, R 343). I produce a certificate—( read Charles Hinchey, convicted June, 1849, and confined one month, and once whipped)—I was present—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18491029-2029]
2029. JAMES MANSFIELD , feloniously assaulting William Towner, with intent to rob him.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM TOWNER . I am a boiler-maker, of Derwent-street, East Greenwich. On Saturday night, 22d Sept., about a quarter-past twelve o'clock, I was alone on the Creek-road, which leads to the Creek-bridge, at Deptford—I had 6 l. within a few halfpence in my waistcoat-pocket—when I was near the bridge, the prisoner came up to me, took hold of my arm, and insisted on my going with him—I looked in his face, and said, "I want nothing of you; you have not been in my company, and I want to have nothing to do with you"—I then turned to avoid him, and got away—he then came up to me suddenly, and said, "You have got money, and I will have it"—I said, "You shall not have it"—I told him the money was my mate's and my own, and I had to take 2 l. to my mate's wife—I got away from him, and he came after me unawares, and caught me somewhere in the ankle and the back of my head, and knocked me down, knelt on me, and kicked me in the nose and mouth, and when I found he would take my life if I did not give him the money, I hallooed "Murder!" and put my hand in my pocket, folded the money up in my hand, and rolled on the side where the money was—the prisoner felt all round the other side of me—Mr. Bond and the policeman then came to my assistance—I received injuries about the loins, the back of my head was cut very much where it fell on the stones; and my eyes, nose, and mouth, were also very much cut by his kicking me—I had never seen him before—he appeared sober.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not see me in your company that night, at Sullivan's public-house? A. No; I did not order some beer there without being able to pay for it—Vickers and Rayman and a cabman had been in my company that night—we were at the Red Cow, the Red Horse, and the Wheatsheaf—we had a quartern of rum and a pint of beer between three of us—we had nothing at the Wheatsheaf—I had left there about five minutes past twelve o'clock—you must have heard them tell me to take the money home, and to take it the first thing in the morning—you were going the same way that I was—you did not wish me good-night—I told you to go away, and I would give you the price of a pot of beer—I did not say," Shove on, you redcoated fool"—the policeman did not come till about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour after you first came—it was a dozen steps off that you came up the second time—you struck me with your foot twice—I swear I had nearly 6 l. in my pocket; 5 l. in gold, and the remainder in silver—I could not have prevented your getting the money if you had known where it was—I had had
one glass of rum at the Red Cow—I had got the money for my work at Kewbridge, and I was going home to my wife.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Where did you part with your two mates? A. At the bottom of Union-street—the prisoner may have been at the Red Cow, and heard what was said about the money, but I did not see him.
CHARLES BOND . I am a poulterer, and live at 3, Church-street, Deptford. On this night I was at my father's house in the Creek-road, and about twenty minutes past twelve o'clock I heard a cry of "Murder!" I ran out, and saw the prisoner kneeling on Towner and striking him—he made a kick at him, but instead of kicking him he kicked me—I immediately pulled the prisoner off—he ran up the road, calling" Police!"—I ran after him, caught him, and with assistance kept him till the police came—he knocked me down and tore my coat—he said if he had his pricker, or dagger, or sticker, he would pot an end to his b—y life—I think he said sticker —Towner had been drinking a little, but knew what he was about.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me kneel on him? A. Yes; and your hands were on his waistcoat-pocket when I pulled you off—I was never convicted, and was never a witness on any occasion—I am in work at the present time—I get 5 s. 6 d. a day, and lose money by coming here.
WILLIAM WELTON ( police-sergeant, R 40). I heard a cry of" Murder! "and" Police! "went in the direction, and found the prisoner in the act of running away—he had his arms up, as if he was going to make a start—I saw Towner bleeding, and all over mud and dirt—he pointed out the prisoner, and said he had assaulted and attempted to rob him—I said he must go with me—he was very obstreperous going along, and I was obliged to get a man and the prosecutor to assist me—the prisoner kicked the prosecutor as we went along, and said he should like to kill the b—r, and said, "If I live to get out of the station I shall mark you; I shall serve you out"—I consider Towner was perfectly sober, but he was so injured that he had the appearance of being not so.
Prisoner. Q. What did I say? A. That you should like to kill him—I suppose you meant Towner and not yourself—you had had some drink, but knew what you were about—the prosecutor was so weak from the injuries that he was led home, passed on from one policeman to another on their beats. Prisoner's Defence. I was going through the streets to my quarters, and met the prosecutor, and asked where I could get some drink; he said, "Go to hell, you b—y soldier!"" Shove on, you red-coated fool! "and used words of a most exciting nature, my temper could not resist it, and I turned back and struck him till he fell; when he was down, he cried" Murder! "and" Robbery!" and that excited my temper; he accuses me because I gave him the blow he deserved.
The prisoner called JAMES PETER QUIN. I am sergeant of the prisoner's regiment, and have known him about eighteen months—his character for honesty is bad. GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Eighteen Months.

Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

2030. MARGARET KING , stealing 1 coat, and other articles, value 17 s. 6 d.; the goods of, Henry Houghton Reyeley, her master: to which she pleaded GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18491029-2031]
2031. THOMAS SMITH , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Peter Cornish, and stealing 1 pocket-book, value 4 d., his property.
PETER CORNISH . I live at 5, John-street, in the parish of St. Olave, Southwark, and am a tailor. On 14th Oct. I left home about nine o'clock in the morning—I left my room door locked; I returned a little after eleven; my room door was still locked—I unlocked it and went in, and saw the prisoner lying in the bed with his clothes on—I asked him what he did there—he said he came there to sleep—I asked how he got in, but that question he would not answer—I found a little drawer was open, and two shillings and a pocket-book had been taken away—the pocket-book was found in the bed, and one shilling laid on the table.
JAMES BURTON ( policeman, M 277). I was called in—I found the prisoner standing by the side of the bed—I saw a female take up the pocket-book between the beds where the prisoner had been lying—the prisoner said he came there to sleep; and he said at the station that he did not steal anything—he was quite sober.
Prisoner's Defence. On that Sunday morning I went to see a party there who lived in the bottom floor; I went in that room and laid down for half an hour. GUILTY —Aged 21.— Confined One Year.

2034. JOSEPH MINCHEM , stealing 5¼lbs. weight of lamb, value 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Head, his master.
MR. BRIARLY conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WARD . I keep the Horse and Groom, in Walworth-road—the prosecutor lives opposite me. On 21st Sept., about twelve o'clock, I saw the prisoner take down a leg of lamb, or mutton, and give it to a woman who swept the crossing—she wrapped it in her apron, and appeared to hesitate which way to go—the prisoner held up his knife, and she went in the direction he pointed out—she returned in about twenty minutes—she did not pay for the lamb.
Cross-examined by MR. COCKLE. Q. Do you know the woman's name? A. I know her by sweeping the crossing—I believe she is well known—I was on the other side of the way when I saw the prisoner take the meat.
WILLIAM HEAD . I am a butcher, and live at the corner of West-street, Walworth-road, opposite Mr. Ward's—the prisoner was in my service nine or ten months. On Friday, 21st Sept., I went to Smithfield, and on my return Mr. Ward told me of this—the prisoner was not then at home—I went to the station, and they sent a policeman with me—the prisoner came home, and I gave him and the woman in charge—I told the prisoner I wanted him to come with me—Mr. Ward had told me he had stolen a leg of lamb—he said he
knew nothing about it; he had not left the front—when he was at the station he told me he had taken the leg of lamb for his Sunday's dinner, and it was at home at his wife's—he told me his residence was in Lock's-fields—I went there and told his wife I had come for a leg of lamb that a woman had left a short time back—she said, "I hope nothing has happened"—I said, "I want the leg of lamb"—the prisoner's son took me to the place.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner has a wife? A. Yes, and eight children—his wages were 15 s. a week, his board, and a joint of meat for his Sunday's dinner—at the time he said that he had not left the front, he was in custody—I never heard that he had been in business for himself—before he entered into my permanent employ he jobbed for me on Fridays and Saturdays, as an extra man—on Sundays he used to take what meat he pleased, in my sight—it is usual amongst butchers to give a bit of meat for dinner on Sundays.
COURT. Q. Was the prisoner one of your salesmen? A. Yes; he was put outside the shop to sell meat, which was put inside to be weighed.
JOHN THOROGOOD ( policeman, P 180). I took the prisoner on 21st Sept.
I told him at the station what he was charged with—he said he knew nothing about it; he had not moved from the front—I asked him where he lived—he said in Lock's-fields—I said it was a wide place; it was no use to seek for it unless he gave some other direction. NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18491029-2035]
2035. JAMES TUGWELL , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Ann Digweed, and stealing 1 half-sovereign, 1 half-crown, and 3 shillings; her property.
ANN DIGWEED . I am a widow, and live in High-street, Lambeth. On the morning of 4th Oct. I was awoke by a noise while I was in my bedroom, the back room on the first-floor—I got up, lita candle, and went up-stairs to my brother—I said I fancied there were thieves in the house—I went down, and found the doors open, which had been shut the night before about half-past twelve o'clock—I saw a ladder against the back-yard door—I did not then perceive that anything was lost—I afterwards missed the money stated—I went through the shop to get a policeman, and in the shop I found a coat and pair of shoes—the policeman found the prisoner on the roof of the next house—he had one coat on but no shoes—he owned that the shoes in the shop were his.
ROBERT DAVIS ( policeman, L 102). I found the prisoner on the roof of the next house—I found a pane of glass had been taken out, and a person had got through the sky-light.
GUILTY .— Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.

2037. EDWARD DRISCOLL , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Lovell, with intent to steal.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH LOVELL . I live at 4, Maryland-buildings, Rotherhithe; my father, James Driscoll, keeps the house. About a quarter before one o'clock, on the 24th Sept., I was awoke by a noise—I threw up the sash and saw the prisoner getting out of the parlour window—he turned and held up his fist, and said, "I am sorry I did not do for the lot of you"—I went down stairs, and found the parlour window open; it had been safe between eight and nine o'clock the night before—the window was shut down, but not fastened—the prisoner lodged next door to us at that time, but the people have removed since he has been in prison.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. YOU say the window was shut the night before? A. Yes, my sister fastened it—my sister has had a few words with the landlady of the house where the prisoner lived—the prisoner was brought back by the policeman immediately.
ELIZA LILLIE . I am the sister of the last witness—I am the wife of James Robert Lillie—he is mate of a ship—I live in the house with my sister—I shut the parlour window shutters between eight and nine o'clock that night—I afterwards heard a noise, and went and awoke my sister.
DAVID FIGG ( policeman, M 33). I was on duty, and heard a cry of" Police!"—I saw the prisoner run out from the court—I asked him what was the matter; he said he did not know—he ran, and I ran and caught him—I said he must come back with me—he said he would not, and resisted violently—with the assistance of a private watchman I took him back—Sarah Lovell saw him and identified him directly—he was sober—he said he would knock my by brains out.
Witness for the Defence. MARIA DRISCOLL. The prisoner lodged with me—I am no relation of his—he has borne a good character—he has been in the habit of coming home late and getting in at the window in my absence—there has been a little quarrel between Mrs. Lillie and me. NOT GUILTY .
